It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. I asked him why he had made such a mess but he denied it. I did not believe him and his not admitting to the \"truth\"  did not help matters. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in heinsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have cause a similar effect. He continued to protest his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in heinsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have cause a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish cause by his father not believing his sone, was to me unforgivable. He continued to protest his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in heinsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have cause a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish cause by his father not believing his son, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in heinsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have cause a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish cause by his father not believing his son, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him. He continued to protest his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in heinsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have cause a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish cause by his father not believing his son, was to me unforgivable. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so you and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him. It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing his son, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing his son, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing his son, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. However, one event does come to mind which might fit this scenario. One evening, when my son was probably around 6 years old he was using the bathroom to take a bath by himself.  After he had finished, and to drain the bath I went upstairs and found the floor and the walls wet with the bathwater.  It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. I asked him why he had made such a mess but he denied it. I did not believe him and his not admitting to the \"truth\"  did not help matters. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing him, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. I asked him why he had made such a mess but he denied it. I did not believe him and his not admitting to the \"truth\"  did not help matters. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing him, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. While I do not mull over this feeling constantly, to me this is one is my biggest mistakes and regret as a father and I sorry for this lapse of judgement. However, one event does come to mind which might fit this scenario. One evening, when my son was probably around 6 years old he was using the bathroom to take a bath by himself.  After he had finished, and to drain the bath I went upstairs and found the floor and the walls wet with the bathwater.  It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. I asked him why he had made such a mess but he denied it. I did not believe him and his not admitting to the \"truth\"  did not help matters. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing him, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. While I do not mull over this feeling constantly, to me this is one is my biggest mistakes and regret as a father and I sorry for this lapse of judgement. However, one event does come to mind which might fit this scenario. One evening, when my son was probably around 6 years old he was using the bathroom to take a bath by himself. It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. However, one event does come to mind which might fit this scenario. One evening, when my son was probably around 6 years old he was using the bathroom to take a bath by himself.  After he had finished, and to drain the bath I went upstairs and found the floor and the walls wet with the bathwater.  It had not been that long since we finished completely renovating the bathroom (tiling, painting, replacing all of the fittings and units, as well as the bath, sink, toilet and shower), so to find the walls so wet was not amusing. I asked him why he had made such a mess but he denied it. I did not believe him and his not admitting to the \"truth\"  did not help matters. Annoyed with this lack of honesty and the potential damage to the new finish on the walls, I removed his Xbox from his room (he would usually play his Xbox before bed) as a punishment. He continued to protest, at length, his innocence but to me the circumstantial evidence was clear. I did not like doing this especially since he seemed so adamant of his innocence which, in hindsight was odd to me. Later it turned out that \"the culprit\" was likely his younger sister.  She had had the bath before him and the water spray pattern on the walls was likely due to her long hair which if wet and she waved it about might have caused a similar effect. I was so ashamed at not believing my boy. While he was not physically harmed in any way, the mental anguish I caused him by not believing him, was to me unforgivable.  I still think about how badly I behaved and how I wish I had did things differently. It was only soapy water after all and I should not have let myself get annoyed at the \"damage\" to the wall.  I still apologise to him now even though he is in his 20's. So although some may regard my actions, not morally wrong, I still regret them and regard it as possibly the worst I have treated him - especially since he was so young and in his formative years. He has grown up to have excellent character and I am so proud of him.  My daughter has never admitted to making the mess (probably far too young to remember anyway). Such a silly thing to argue over but it still fills me with regret and shame that I did not believe him. While I do not mull over this feeling constantly, to me this is one is my biggest mistakes and regret as a father and I sorry for this lapse of judgement. I do have to state that this was a one-off and that I am not so petty to get worked up about such small things.  Again whilst this might not align with theseverity of some of the stories that you might receive, I feel this fits within the scope of my character. </p>"},{"timestamp":"16:26:08","text":"<p>Reading this question it is difficult for me to think of a time when I did something as bad as \"morally\" wrong.
